I am informed that today's meeting cannot be attended by the following Members: Mrs Janja Attack, Mrs Janja Klasinc, Mrs Ludmila Novak, Mr Samo Bevk, Mr Bojan Starman, Mr Brane Golubovic, Mr Branko Kurnjek – is here – from 16.15 onwards, until then he is with us, Mr Zvonko Černač, Mr Marijan Pojbič. I welcome everyone here. We're moving to 11. On the other hand, this is a proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the law on agricultural collection of Slovenia in the framework of the ordinary procedure. The draft law was tabled by a group of six Members and Members with the first-signed Jacob the Intercessor. With regard to this proposal, the Committee on Agriculture, Forestry, Food and the Environment proposes to the National Assembly that it be decided that the proposal for a law is not suitable for further consideration. I give the floor to the chairman of the Committee, Jakob President, to further explain the proposal for a decision.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u2	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. Madam Secretary of State with co-workers, colleagues and colleagues! The Committee on Agriculture, Forestry, Food and the Environment considered the Proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on the Agricultural Forestry Chamber of Slovenia as a working body at its 27th session. This year's 11th. In addition to the draft law, the members of the Committee also received an opinion from the Legal and Legislative Service, which made some specific comments to the draft law, which were summarised in its amendments by the Slovenian People's Party's Group as the drafter of the law. The Committee also took note of the opinion of the Government of 11 July, in which the Government notes that the proposal for a law can be supported, but provided that Article 9 which allows the temporary financing of the Chamber is provided so that, in the event that the Chamber submits an annual work programme by the beginning of the current year but does not yet obtain the consent of the competent minister, the regular tasks of the public services shall be continuously carried out in the current year, on the basis of a decision of the competent minister. In its amendment, the Government also proposed deleting Article 12, which lays down the obligation of the Consultative Referendum and provides for certain provisions on compulsory membership in the agri-agricultural assembly system. The Committee also received an opinion from the Commission of the State Council for Agriculture, Forestry and Food, which does not support the proposal. The Committee took note of the opinion of the Chamber of Agriculture and Economy of Slovenia, which in principle does not oppose the proposed reduction of membership in the Chamber, and notes that the income of the Chamber for natural persons would also be reduced as a result of the removal of small farms from compulsory membership. Their estimate is estimated at around 500 000 euros. Even before discussing individual articles and amendments and amendments to the Committee's amendments, the Member of the Parliament of the Positive Slovenia Group proposed that the Committee, in accordance with Article 128 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, discuss and decide on a proposal for a decision that the proposal for a law would not be suitable for further consideration. In particular, he based his proposal on the fact that there have been substantial changes in the calculation of the determination of cadastral income, which is the criterion for membership in the Chamber, since the introduction of the bill into the legislative procedure. While the majority of the members expressed support for the proposed decision, they pointed out that the law in force should undoubtedly be amended or a new system law should be drafted as soon as possible to ensure the normal functioning of the Chamber. Those members of the committee who opposed the proposed decision also agreed that it was necessary to amend the applicable law as soon as possible, so they called on the government representatives to speed up the drafting of a new law in cooperation with the Agriculture and Agriculture Chamber. Some members of coalition parliamentary groups expressed the expectation that the new law prepared by the Government would be based on voluntary membership, since they felt that the public services in the Chamber would not be at any risk because they were largely financed by the budget and that the Chamber would be more receptive in this way to the needs of its membership. The representative of the Coalition Group also stressed that legal persons who own agricultural land or are engaged in agricultural, forestry or fishing activities should be derived from compulsory membership in the Chamber. After the discussion on the draft law was concluded, the Committee adopted the following decision with 7 votes in favour and 1 against: Proposal for a Law amending and supplementing the Law on the Agricultural Forestry Chamber of Slovenia is not suitable for further consideration. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u3	Thank you, too. I would like to give the floor to the representative of the proposal for a bill to Mr Jakob Preseknik for the introduction of the position on the proposal of the home working body. Here we go again, Mr. President.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u4	Thank you very much again for your word. That's why I just stayed for this pay phone. In opening, I should like to stress, on behalf of the applicants, that the Agricultural Forestry Chamber Act was adopted by the National Assembly on 20 May 1999 in order to bring together the membership of the two groups. The first group shall cover natural persons who are owners, tenants, users and holders of the right to use agricultural land and forests in the territory of the Republic of Slovenia and persons who carry out agricultural, forestry, fishing activities and have notified themselves as independent entrepreneurs, and persons who, as owners, tenants or users of an agricultural or forestry establishment, carry out an agricultural, forestry or fishing activity as their sole or principal business, are not in possession of a cadastral income and are liable for an activity tax. The second group shall cover legal persons registered as agricultural organisations. In addition to both, there are voluntary members of the Chamber who may be legal or natural persons. Following the adoption of this law, the law was still amended, with the amendment of the law reducing the number of members years ago in such a way that the smallest owners who did not have a full interest in participating in the Chamber were thus ousted. Thus, something similar was observed during the spring period that some of the reasons why a change of law should be made in the chamber. In this amendment of the law, we have tried to resolve the following issues in particular. Firstly, the criteria for membership in the Chamber of Agriculture and Agriculture have already been mentioned, in such a way that, in addition to the level of the cadastral income, the condition for compulsory membership is also the entry into the register of agricultural holdings. In particular, we wanted to ensure that the members of the Chamber were those receiving the subsidies or all those involved in the agricultural policy measures. However, this cannot be achieved directly, so we said that all those who are registered in the register of agricultural holdings. In this way, in our opinion, the number of members of the chamber from a good 108 000 would be reduced to around 78 000, thus limiting to all those actually associated with agriculture. This means that the members of the Chamber are owners, tenants, users, right to use or users of agricultural land and forests in the territory of Slovenia and are registered registers of agricultural holdings. But the catastrophe income for membership is limited as it was before, and we have not made any changes. Due to such a reduced number of members, we also propose a change in the composition or a reduction in the number of members of the Councils. We further propose deleting the permanent arbitration of the Chamber. Given that the members of the Chamber do not conclude contracts with each other and that the arbitration is pointless or at all irrelevant. The proposal seeks to regulate the possibility of temporary funding of public services in the field of public agricultural advisory services until all documents between the Chamber and the Government are agreed or signed. It is a system of twelve, such as any other public service financed by the State budget. We proposed an invitation to a consultation referendum on the abolition of mandatory membership in the Chamber, which would be held among those members of the Chamber who would remain members of the Chamber on the basis of the new criteria for membership in the Chamber. However, when amending the law, we have pointed out that, in view of the anticipated changes in the definition of cadastral income, the law with the corresponding amendments will have to be further supplemented. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u6	Dear President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! The government did not oppose the bill but proposed, as already mentioned, two amendments. We therefore support temporary funding, but under the condition of a preliminary submission at least a draft programme and a ministerial decision which can provide funding not after twelve but up to twelve. The second amendment referred to Article 12 of the bill providing for an obligation to consult a referendum on compulsory membership in the Chamber. The Government has proposed that this Article should be deleted because it considers it inappropriate for the legislator to prescribe a mandatory consultation referendum within the framework of the membership of the Chamber, since this is an internal matter of the Chamber and since such verification of the wishes of members relating to compulsory membership can be established within the framework of the functioning of the Chamber itself. On the other hand, the enforcement of the interests of those engaged in agriculture or representing the interests of farmers and agricultural organisations and the impact of agricultural policies on the promotion of development. Comments are about establishing criteria for compulsory membership, one is the criterion in force, that is the cadastral income, and the other is that many members of chambers are not directly linked to agricultural activity. The current criterion causes and will be too abnormal with changes in the cadastral income. ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u22	Thank you very much, President. Nicely greeted! You have an extremely important marriage ahead of you, and you could even say a revolutionary law that, in the middle and long term, could reveal a lot of what is now hidden, and it should have been exposed long ago. The Public Information Access Act was adopted in 2003 and has already brought a considerable revolution in the public sector by providing access to all information on the functioning of public entities, with the exception of tax exemptions, i.e. documents which these entities are not obliged to disclose. The law has brought about a major change in the philosophy of the public sector. If the State had previously been considered to be operating in secret to the public, it had to act in an open and transparent manner. The law of the week, Gantar's marriage could have been called, meant a great step forward. Later the law was amended several times and each next step went towards even greater transparency, such as the 2006 novel, which provided for certain data to be absolutely public, so that they cannot be subject to exceptions, such as data on public servants and public officials, even though they are personal data, are 100 % public if they relate to their work. According to international research, according to a study by Access Info and the Canadian Institute for Law and Democracy, Slovenia is among the first three countries in the world in terms of transparency in the public sector. But it's never so good that it couldn't be any better, and it's time for the next step. This next step is to extend transparency to undertakings owned directly or indirectly by the State and the municipalities or under the direct or indirect dominant influence of the State or municipalities. These companies are our common assets and are turning billions of euros in our common money. All too often, their overseers and their board members act as if it were their property, as if it were their own feud, which no one cares about, is really our common property. We therefore rightly expect these companies to operate economically, to make profits for us and to operate transparently. These companies must be under the thorough scrutiny of the media and the public. According to the Ernst&Young survey carried out this year, Slovenia has landed in an infamous first place after the level of corruption in the economy. This assessment relates primarily to publicly owned enterprises, state or municipality property. According to the IMD scale, World Competitiveness Yearbook is Slovenia, following corporate governance, 51st place from the 59th countries they assess. Expansion of transparency to publicly owned enterprises results in public oversight of their business and consequently more cost-effective and responsible management of these companies, the provision of a better, healthier business environment, in particular for small and medium-sized enterprises that can compete under fairer and more competitive conditions for business with these companies, the reduction of corruption risks and the transparency of the remuneration of managers and more transparent staffing. Respected colleagues and colleagues, in companies owned by a majority of the state or municipality, will make public all donor and sponsoring contracts, all legal and advisory contracts, salaries, emoluments and other ratings of members of the administrations and members of the supervisory boards. It will be possible to see with whom and what business these companies are doing. In the proposed amendments, we have taken a balanced approach so as not to compromise the functioning of businesses on the market. About that a little bit later. In particular, the strict regime will apply to companies with 100 per cent state ownership. They will be subject to the so-called pro-active transparency, which means that all their transactions will be published online, according to the domestic statement of the current Supervisor, which concerns only a narrower public sector, will also extend to enterprises owned by state, local communities. So much for the intro. Later in the debate, however, much more.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u23	Thanks for the introduction. We are moving on to a presentation on the views of the groups of Members. Mag. Majda Potter will present the position of the Group of Social Democrats.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u24	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Mr. Secretary with the co-workers! When considering the proposed law, when it comes to changes and additions to the basic law, the Social Democrats are, in particular, reminded of what happens when they are treated on a regular basis. The initial proposal of the law, which received both the inter-resort coordination procedures and the public presentation – the tribunal, for example, was organised in this regard – where different stakeholders could make their comments, is the way that leads to the amended law, where the interested and professional public had the opportunity to comment. This is all the more important when it comes to the availability of information of a public nature in a law that I do not say expected and urgent. Why is this marriage so important? It has already been mentioned that the basic law of 2003 has also been an important step forward in the opening of data for citizens and citizens, bringing us closer to the fact that the State is a state institution because of people. And the right to access public information is a fundamental human right, as enshrined in the Constitution, in the second paragraph of Article 39, and this right constitutes a basis for the open, transparent and responsible functioning of public administration. This is of crucial importance to people. And it also follows that goal set by the government of Alenka Bratušek for a purpose; i.e. to increase the confidence of citizens and citizens in the functioning of the state and its institutions. The proposed novel is intended to identify new public information commitments, to establish an official register of IJPES debtors and to set up centralised and uniform pro-active public disclosures of certain debtors, which will be achieved through the Administration of the Republic of Slovenia for public payments. Social Democrats also adopt the objective of this law, which is defined as raising the degree of transparency in making business more efficient to manage and finance business entities under the dominant influence of public law. We are not plowing here, but this goal is similar with solutions in some other countries, such as Croatia, Great Britain, Denmark, Sweden, Serbia or even remote New Zealand. The solutions should help to create competitive market conditions, especially for small and medium-sized companies, especially when they want to operate with companies under the dominant influence of the State or of the local self-government communities. It is therefore a matter of ensuring a more competitive business environment. The second objective is to ensure the transparency of the remuneration of managers in state-owned companies and municipalities and to create conditions in which recruitment in these companies will be transparent. This should also lead to the objective of reaching individual leadership positions by people with adequate experience and quality leadership. All of this will also reduce corruption risks. However, it should also be stressed that only specific key information is identified as public information for new entrants, that new entrants are not burdened with administrative burdens and that the pro-active publication of absolute public expenditure data should avoid any burden on new entrants. This is to be achieved by means of a centralised and uniform way of publication. It is important for us that the applicant has taken into account the comments made by the interested public, but we have not overlooked this information that part of the comments, in particular the part which the applicant has failed to take into account, has promised to abolish in the next step when it also takes over the Public Sector Information Re-use Directive in 2014. Well, I should also like to draw attention to the comments of the information officer, which are taken into account in the proposal to a large extent, but we have not overlooked that the information officer points out and draws attention to some inappropriate solutions in another law, which is in the Slovenian State Holding Act. ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u25	Thank you, too. Ms Kristina Valenčič will move forward with the position of the Parliamentary Group of the National Gazette.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u26	. Mr President, the Honourable Minister with colleagues, colleagues and colleagues! Article 39 of our Constitution in the second paragraph reads: "Everyone has the right to receive information of a public nature for which the law has a legitimate interest, except in cases provided for by law." And we are talking about the extent and manner of exercising this right of our citizens and citizens, as well as all other persons in the territory of the Republic of Slovenia, in the consideration of the novel which is before us today. To understand what this right contains, we must first know what information of a public nature actually means. It is provided for in Article 4 of the current law, which states that this is any information originating from the work area of the Authority, but exists as a document, case, file, record register or documentary material produced by the Authority itself, in cooperation with another body or obtained from other persons. The purpose of such an arrangement is to ensure the public and openness of public authorities, which are both consumers and users of public funds. And since taxpayers contribute to public finances, it is right to have the possibility and, of course, also the right to control the use of these funds. This is also intended to make public information accessible, but information of a public nature is subject to the provision of only public administrations under the current regime, but we know that public or public financial resources are not available and managed by public administration authorities, but also by companies in which the state, state authorities or local communities have a dominant influence. It is therefore right to monitor how public funds are spent, subject to such companies as well; and this is the main content of the amendment of the law. The practice shows that these companies are exposed to major corruption risks in terms of irrational spending, financial exhaustion of the company, questionable recapitalisations and poor management by public funds. On the other hand, such companies may also be favoured or in a monopolistic position in front of other economic operators, which is detrimental to the competitiveness of the latter and does not contribute to a healthy business environment. However, as economic operators are concerned, they will be obliged to provide public access to specific key information, namely information on concluded legal transactions relating to expenditure and real estate management, and information related to the level of remuneration and credit ratings, and data related to the recruitment of managers. The status of information of a public nature may also, following the novelty, obtain a specific category of information if it is given the dominant public interest to disclose it. The proposed legal solutions in the National Gazette are understood as contributing to greater transparency in terms of spending budgetary resources, which also means a step towards restoring the confidence of our citizens in our country's institutions and policies; a contribution to greater transparency in state-owned business operations, which also means their lower dependence on every day's current policy; and a step towards improving a healthy business environment. Not only the public and the media, including individual institutions such as the KPK, the Court of Auditors, the Commissioner for Access to Public Information, the AUKN and others have for a long time been alerting the need for transparent and public access to state-owned companies, to which a maximum degree of market and citizen confidence in the sound and sound functioning of these companies is being established. In the Civil Gazette, we support all efforts and activities in the fight against corruption and economic crime. We are confident that the proposed solutions will greatly help to reduce the risks of possible abuses, corruption and customerism, so of course they have our support.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u27	Thank you. Mr Michael Prevc will present the position of the Slovenian People's Party's Group.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u28	Thank you very much for your words, Mr. President. A respected minister with colleagues, respectable colleagues and colleagues! We have before us an extensive novel of the Law on Access to Public Information, which comes to the hearing of the National Assembly in a very interesting time. Yesterday, we adopted changes in the field of political parties that limit their funding and should allow for greater transparency and reduction of corruption risks. More than that, however, the proposed law has been subject to political interference around the novel of the Slovenian State Holding Act, to which we have been witnessing for quite some time and according to the Slovenian People's Party's Group for much too long. Why did I point out exactly these two amendments to the legislation? The Law on Political Parties, because it essentially assessed transparency increases, is similar to the proposed amendment to the Public Information Access Act and the amendment to the Slovenian State Holding Act because it is inseparably linked to the Act under consideration. The proposed law has one essential objective, namely to increase transparency in the functioning of business entities under the dominant influence of public law persons, in particular companies owned directly or indirectly by the State. Such an increase in transparency would help to more effectively manage the assets and financial resources of those entities, which, of course, we welcome in the Slovenian People's Party's Group. Nevertheless, we remain on solid ground, as in the past there have been numerous rules that have already been addressed and adopted, which have promised the same, and in the end there is not much left of all high-flying words. The proposaler, i.e. the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, points out in the proposed material that, I quote, "...state enterprises are favoured in front of all other companies, creating poor competitive conditions for the remaining companies on the market and the lame functioning of mainly small and medium-sized enterprises." And it continues, I quote, "These reasons dictate the adoption of legislative measures that will help to increase the transparency of public companies and companies that are closely linked to the public sector. Through transparency and the establishment of direct responsibility against individuals, this is primarily for citizens and residents of the Republic of Slovenia, and there will also be less dependence on the impact of the current policy." End of quote. Interesting findings, especially in the fact that in the government and coalition we have a number of state-owned fighters who, in the past, some still have advocated state ownership as the only rescue, considered management to be the most effective, and compared only the indication of its privatisation even with high treason. The purpose of the proposed law is therefore to enhance transparency of business, public commitment, responsibility and integrity in the management of economic public institutions, public undertakings and businesses and other private legal persons under the dominant influence of the Republic of Slovenia, local self-government communities and other public law entities by establishing new public information obligations. The new entrants are thus, I quote, "... an economic company and other private legal persons under direct or indirect dominant influence, individually or collectively, the Republic of Slovenia, the self-government local communities and other persons of public law." End of quote. Simply put, these are all economic operators where the country or municipalities have a direct or indirect ownership share of more than 50%. The public will thus become information from the concluded legal business relating to the acquisition, disposal or management of the actual assets of a business entity or the expenditure of a business entity, such as the procurement of goods, works, agent and advisory services, sponsoring, donor and authorising contracts. In addition, information of a public nature will also provide information concerning membership, remuneration and ratings and the recruitment of members of management and supervisory authorities and other legal representatives of a business entity. Exceptions will be business secrets, personal data and the like. We do not, in principle, object to this in the Slovenian People's Party's Group, but we wonder how such disclosures, although with some restrictions, will affect possible sales procedures for such economic operators. We know that sales procedures for major companies are complex, mainly based on business secrets and other sensitive data. However, with such disclosures and such transparency, which may be in the public interest, many may also question the procedure. In addition, the proposed law also makes it possible to make available to the public various personnel information such as information on the amount of agreed or paid remuneration or creditworthiness of a member of the management body, the managing authority, the other legitimate representative of a business entity, the member of the supervisory authority, and information relating to the recruitment of those persons from which the conditions and criteria for the recruitment of a particular function are evident. The public will thus be able to learn about the payment made to the management person immediately after the payment and not only once a year with the data for the previous year, or it will be possible to know in advance the agreed amount of the remuneration of the management person, that is, before this remuneration has been paid. This should also be important for agreed discharge amounts, since prior public awareness is essential to allow for controls on the adequacy and appropriateness of the intended remuneration. These changes should also help to create competitive market conditions, particularly for small and medium-sized companies wishing to operate or compete with companies under the dominant influence of the State or of the local self-government. To create a healthier business environment where competition will be free and the same market conditions for all will apply. In addition, the proposed law also establishes a register of public information committed to public information which will be made publicly and online. In addition to the commitments made so far, new undertakings will be included, i.e. business entities under the dominant influence of public law. Centralised communication of information of a public nature, a global network, through the recording of expenditure data, and certain administrative burdens relating to the implementation of the Public Information Access Act for new debtors is also possible. The proposed Act on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on Access to Public Information therefore brings a lot of changes that can severely shake the functioning of companies in majority state ownership, which we consider to be positive in the Slovenian People's Party's Group, and we will not object to this law at first reading. Thank you.

In order not to impose new administrative burdens on the debtors, the publication of absolute public expenditure data will be provided without any burden on new debtors, in a centralised and uniform manner through the Public Payments Authority. Although in practice that expenditure contract will still, in principle, be protected as a business secret, the amendment of the Public Information Access Act will clearly identify those data which are absolutely public and cannot, as a rule, represent business secrecy. This is a sensitive area where it is particularly important to analyse the possible negative effects on a competing entity and on the conclusion of legal transactions. ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u36	Thank you. Mr President, Minister with fellow Members, Members and Members of Parliament! The proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on Access to Public Information was forwarded to the Government for consideration and adoption by the National Assembly 7. Ten. 2013 The right to information of a public nature is a fundamental human right guaranteed in Article 39(2) of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia and constitutes a key means in the fight for the open, transparent and responsible functioning of public administration. This right has already been clarified by the adoption of the Public Information Access Act in 2003 and supplemented both in 2005 and 2006, which has been mentioned several times in previous discussions. The mere exercise of the right to information of a public nature does not, of course, achieve that the public administration would be fully open and would ensure that its work is made public in all respects. In practice, as the drafter has also stated, the shortcomings of the rules in force are identified in particular as regards the cycle of bodies bound. The current law also binds only bodies that manage and carry out public tasks, but not all those that manage public funds or finance owned by the State, self-government local communities or other persons governed by public law. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we have repeatedly pointed out that companies in which the state or state authorities have the dominant influence should also be involved in the circle of the debtors, and in particular that banks in which the state has the dominant influence, or banks in which the controlled liquidation or bankruptcy is carried out, should also be brought to justice by means of all citizens and citizens or taxpayers. Other authorities have also pointed to this problem. In particular, it seems to us that it is not possible to obtain data on illegal banking transactions in which State property is damaged, or data relating to suspected crimes, the granting of bad credits in so-called tycoon stories, and access to such data is denied even to the investigating commission of the National Assembly. In our Group of Members, we welcome the extension of the circle of debtors to economic societies and other private legal persons under direct or indirect dominant influence, individually or collectively, of self-government communities and other persons of public law. We also support the establishment of a public and online register of public information debtors to ensure, in particular, the transparency of the public information obligations through this portal, which will include the debtors under the law currently in force and new entrants or business entities under the dominant influence of public law. That is why we welcome these solutions. An assessment of the financial implications of the draft law on the State budget and other public funds suggests that the amendment of the law will result in costs for the operation of an information officer or an authorised person, given the new additional employment that will be required, amounting to EUR 35,000. Given that we are in favour of a weak and effective public administration or public administration in our Group of Members, we propose that the Information Commissioner should not be given a new job, but rather that an appropriate transfer of a public servant or staff member from another State authority be made, which must, of course, meet all the conditions laid down to ensure that there is no increase in the number of employees in the State Administration. Finally, I would like to point out that in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Group, of course, we support all the objectives set by this law, namely transparency of business, public commitment, responsibility and integrity in governance, so we will not oppose the proposed solutions in the first reading. Luka Koper, a sponsoring contract with a local club to actually promote this local club Luka Koper, which is a monopolist. What added value is that now? And vice versa. State monopolies spend money on advertising, propaganda and so on. Wrong world. And this is the added value that this new law brings. Thank you very much.

The adoption of the new law is mainly due to the requirement to implement the European acquis into Slovenian law, namely Directive 2010–2018. The Republic of Slovenia is already in the pre-trial procedure due to the non-notification of national rules for the transposition of the parental leave directive. At the end of May this year, we received an official reminder from the European Commission. The deadline for implementing this Directive was March 2013. That is how we now have this need to comply with the European Union Directive. At the same time, this bill also aligns terminology with international documents, which we also wanted to bring users closer to the law itself and make it more understandable. For example, we now have a name, we are talking about maternity leave, paternity leave and leave for the care and protection of the child. Now in this bill, maternity leave is called maternity leave because it is intended for mothers, paternity leave remains a paternity leave, and leave for care and child protection is renamed parental leave because it is actually a right for parents to share it with each other. The same applies to the right of a gift at the birth of a newborn, which will also apply to adoptive parents and guardians. This seems to me to be a really important novelty and the acquisition of this law. The amended law provides for 15 days of parental leave as before, and a further 15 days of paid paternity leave are added. We therefore support the amendment of the law in order to help eliminate inequalities. At the same time, we are pleased, as I have already said, that the proposal before us also removes a number of administrative obstacles. We will support the proposal to conclude that the law is suitable for further consideration. Thank you.

A completely new proposal for a law on parental protection and family benefits will, we hope in the Positive Slovenia Group, replace more than a decade old law that currently governs this area. Today, it is extremely important to talk precisely about objective conditions, since they include economic conditions that depend on the employment of the younger generation, which provides them with financial security, thus the basic conditions for living, protecting children and others. In this context, we can talk about the integration content of individual parts of the family policy based on the social role of the country and the fact that it must not overlook the fundamental social cell, that is, the family. The drafting of the law took months, coordinated with a number of social partners and other interested public. However, this is primarily due to the implementation of several directives of the European Union. Remember, during the public debate, the most raised was the question of paternity leave, which, with its non-transferable 30 days, would affect the mother's right to care for her child for one year at a time. We are pleased that with the competent ministry and other interested public, we have managed to find a solution that removes all doubts about the appropriateness of involving the father in the upbringing and care of the child. And what is the solution? The draft law fully provides for a new system of leave arrangements at the birth of a child. Maternal leave, before maternity leave, remains 105 days, of which 15 days are compulsory. And parental leave, formerly leave for care and protection, which is an individual and, in principle, non-transferable right of parents. Each parent has the right to parental leave for 130 days and can pass 100 days of parental leave on to his mother, and 30 days is intolerable. Dad can take 130 days of parental leave to his mother. With this solution, we have been satisfied with the non-transferable 30 days following the European Directive for Mother, and this is provided in the form of a father's leave. The draft law provides for 15 days of paternity leave, as regulated at this time, at the birth of the baby together with the mother, and an additional 15 days of paid paternity leave after the parental leave has expired after one year. This means that the total duration of leave at birth is extended from 12 to 12.5 months. For the purpose of paternity leave is the presence of a father associated with the child in the care and protection of the child. In the Parliamentary Group Positive Slovenia we stress the importance of provisions that better regulate rights for social parents as they follow social development and various forms of family. These provisions are also followed by a directive of the European Union requiring from national countries a uniform regulation of parental rights for adoptive parents or persons who actually care for a child to be cared for, cared for and protected. The provisions of the law therefore regulate the following rights: parental leave for adopters, which is determined in the same way as parental leave for biological parents. This compares the rights at the time of the birth and adoption of the child in a way that empowers the adoptors or persons entrusted with the child to the care and education of the child for the purpose of adoption to have the right to parental leave. A paternity leave may also benefit another person who actually nurtures and protects a child on the basis of a valid enforcement address in accordance with the rules governing family relations. These are foster parents, foster parents, caregivers, mothers’ spouse, mothers’ extramarital partner, and others who actually spend their time caring for the child. All these persons can use paternity leave only if the biological father of the child does not use it. The bill also extends the range of beneficiaries to shorter working hours and payments of contributions in the case of four or more children. The proposed provisions therefore increase the benefits of those children who are not cared for by parents. Persons who actually care for a child may be granted the right to a shorter working time and the payment of contributions in the case of four or more children. The proposal also extends the range of beneficiaries to support the birth of a child. This right may also be exercised by another person and the adoptive parent, unless this right has already been exercised by one of the parents of the child. Another person may also have the right to a large family allowance in cases where three or more children from the same family live without parents. In view of social development and different family situations, the legal provisions also extend the range of beneficiaries of the partial loss of income payment. This right shall also be extended to another person if he is actually taking care of the child. The purpose of this right is to enable parents or other persons who wish to take care of a child at home with a severe mental disability or a severely displaced child to leave the labour market and therefore receive a partial payment for lost income and appropriate inclusion in social security. Since the primary purpose of child care is to protect the child, it is only right that other persons are entitled to this right. In general, I can agree that the basic objective of the proposal is to maximize the prosecution of children's benefits, to promote a more equitable burden-sharing between parents in child and home care, to facilitate reconciliation between professional and family life, to ensure the universality of family benefits, to ensure freedom of choice in the way in which parental protection rights are exercised, to maximize justice and social equality, or to ensure the most stable environment for families, to establish a system of evaluation of the situation in the field of family policy, and to provide further information. More appropriate and effective solutions are likely to be able to be highlighted in the debate itself. Let me conclude by saying that in Positive Slovenia we will support the conclusion that the proposal for the Parental Protection and Family Benefits Act is suitable for further consideration. ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u86	All welcome, Madam Vice-President of the National Assembly, distinguished Madam Minister with co-workers, Members and Members! The Law on Parental Protection and Family Benefits was adopted in 2001 and was then updated and amended in 2003 and 2006. Changes in social as well as demographic matters have undoubtedly led to the need for a reformed law, as well as the requirements of the European directives. The justification for changes and innovations will be shown or will show further consideration of the law in the legislative process, with every comment and controversy that will come from the professional public and civil society in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Group. The maternity leave will replace maternity leave and there remain 105 days, as today, of which 15 days are mandatory. Parental leave had previously been a leave for the care and protection of the child, each of the parents would have the right to parental leave for 130 days, with the mother being able to pass 100 days of parental leave on to the father, and 30 days is intolerable. The father can pass on 130 days of parental leave to the mother, in this way we can satisfy the conditions required by the European Directive for the mother and, for the father, we can satisfy the conditions of the father's 30-day leave, which is also non-transferable. At the time of the birth of the new law, there were aspirations for the father to make compulsory use of his part of parental leave for at least 30 days, as was the proposal of the ministry or government. It makes more sense for us to propose that parents can communicate their part of parental leave to their partners. This is a specific advantage in professions or in jobs, when a mother or father has, to say, domestically, better work and thus better pay, builds a career, provides a smooth work process and could then also affect family and family budget relations. At times, though, a father is a more capable caregiver or a more loving parent and has the opportunity to spend parental leave on him. The bill foresees 15 days of paternity leave as before — at the birth of a child, together with a mother — and an additional 15 days of paid paternity leave after the parental leave after one year. As we have heard in the future, the total holiday at birth will extend from one year to 12 months and a half. In addition, the bill seeks to reduce the administrative burden on employers by exempting employers from certain requirements, such as the fulfilment of the basis for the benefit of allowances and the notification of the transfer of such leave. The work of social work centres should also be simplified, as the law provides for solutions in some places that are simpler and cheaper, such as the harmonisation of deadlines and the ordinary postal delivery, and we must note that this can reduce the legal security of the exercise of the rights of persons in the process. We also find it questionable to delete the possibility of applying the child supplement back. The law provides for the right to family benefits for children under the age of 18, except for the right to an allowance for the care of the child and an allowance for a large family. Rights linked to the age of 8 years, however, the proposal for a law should be uniform until the first grade of primary school is completed. Although the solution is practical at first sight, the oldest child in a multi-child family is already attending primary school, but the proposal of the law ignores that certain children still need an escort to school and home. For 8 years, after an old elementary school system in an eight-year-old age, it was the final first grade, and now it's the sixth year. We do not agree with the proposaler's statement that it is at all important, in certain circumstances, that the law already in force regulated the rights to parental protection and family benefits at an enviable level, in comparison with other countries of the European Union, and that the solutions in the proposed new law constitute an upgrade of existing rights. This is true, but it must be borne in mind that the long-term trends in Slovenia's nativity are negative and that special attention should be paid to rights in the area of parental protection and family benefits, not so much in terms of increasing financial resources as in terms of the effectiveness of family benefits, in order to enable the family to decide on family policy as independently as possible of the social or economic situation, although this will never be possible altogether. Again, we need to mention jobs; it is said, due to the lack of jobs and the problem of youth unemployment. The unemployed do not hope to create a family or have children. Thus, the mother’s first birth takes place only at the age of thirty, or after thirty, or later. It is also a very important programme of housing policy for young people, which is currently insufficiently addressing the housing problem of young families or couples wishing to have children. The range of insured persons shall be extended in accordance with the applicable arrangements due to amendments to other regulations such as the Pension and Disability Insurance Act and the Wages Regulation and other remuneration of public servants for work abroad. Parental protection insurers are all persons who are necessarily involved in pension and disability insurance. The right to compensation at the time of maternity leave and the right to payment of social security contributions at the time of maternity leave is a novelty, the provision of the Labour Relations Act stipulating the right to a maternity leave. The right to compensation at the time of maternity leave was proposed and adopted already during the previous government. The law no longer regulates adoption leave because adopters have the right to parental leave, which we think is a suitable solution. The mother has the right to maternity leave for a period of 105 days, which does not change with the proposal of the law. The duration is also consistent with the 1992 Council Directive in force on the introduction of measures to promote improvements in the safety and health of pregnant workers and workers who have recently given birth or breast-feeding, which provides for a minimum period of maternity leave at least 14 weeks prior to and after childbirth. Father’s leave is the right of the father to be present during the earliest period of the child’s care and protection. However, use is possible for 15 days at the beginning and, as already mentioned, even after one year, the remainder, which will also be paid gradually. After the current law, the parents were jointly entitled to 260 days of parental leave, and all of them will now be able to agree on the method of use for these 260 days. This means that the entire leave for care and protection has so far been transferable, which I think will be almost entirely from now on. With the bill, the right to parental leave has become the individual right of each parent of the 130 days, the mother must keep 30 days in order to be sufficient for the directive, while the father, as we said, can pass all 130 days on to the mother, and he is satisfied with the directive with this compulsory paternity leave. Parental leave should be used immediately after the end of maternity leave. This ensures continuous care and protection of the child, as there is no interruption between the use of maternity leave and parental leave. There are reasons why one parent or both parents are not entitled to parental leave. In view of the arrangements so far, there are several reasons. Both apply when a child is entrusted with the care and training of another person, or is placed in a foster family, or the institution and parents are not entitled to parental leave because they do not actually care and protect the child. In such cases, the other person entrusted with the child’s upbringing and care, or the foster child’s placement, will use parental leave to care for and protect the child. One parent does not have the right to parental leave in cases where the child is entrusted with the care and training of the other parent or lives with only the other parent who protects and cares for the child. In the event that he has left the child, in the event that he is deprived of his parental right, in the event that his/her contact with the child is prohibited in accordance with the rules governing family relations and in the event that he/she has been given a measure prohibiting the approximation in accordance with the rules governing the duties and powers of the police and the prevention of domestic violence. It is a new way of exploiting a transferred parental leave. It is no longer possible to make use of this leave after days, but only in a narrow sequence, and it is not necessary to make full use of the leave at the same time. From the point of view of simplifying the procedures on the Centre’s part in excluding compensation from the point of view of the organisation of the work process on the part of employers, the proposed solution is more appropriate than it has been so far. Parents must inform the Centre of their unused leave no later than 15 days after the performance so that the beneficiary can pay the compensation in due time. This is a novelty, since it was previously an obligation of the employer. On the basis of the newly established basis for calculating the remuneration at the time of the transfer of leave, the employer of the centre is no longer obliged to report the days of the use of the leave and the data for the calculation of the basis, which is one of the removals of administrative barriers on the part of employers. The record of the use of the holiday is maintained by the centre. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, we find that certain solutions are appropriate and at the same time sufficient for European directives, so we will not object to the fact that the proposal for a law is suitable for further consideration. However, in further consideration of the law, we will closely monitor the purpose and objectives of the proposed solutions and in cases where it will prove that the legislative provisions do not follow the very purpose and objectives of the Government, including their solutions. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u104	Thank you for your word, Madam Vice President. Dear Madam Minister, colleague and colleague! The rights governed by the Proposal for a Law on Parental Protection and Family Benefits are part of the measures of a uniform family policy. This is implemented in our country and is based on the idea of the social role of the country, so the state cannot overlook a fundamental social cell such as the family. The purpose of parental protection rights is to ensure equal opportunities and to prevent any discrimination against parents, especially mothers, in the labour market. Parental protection contributes significantly to the elimination of differences between women and men in the labour market and promotes a more equitable sharing of child protection and care obligations between women and men. The current arrangement allows parents, especially women, to be as well coordinated as family and professional life. And given that Slovenia is still very uneven in the level of employment of women in the European Union at the summit, the division of unpaid work between partners is still very uneven in existing legislation. Inequality is also reflected in the use of parental protection insurance rights. Although both parents have this right, women are those who mostly benefit from parental leave and part - time work. According to some studies, the difference in time spent by partners on homework increases with the arrival of a child. In Positive Slovenia, we are particularly pleased with the following solutions brought by the proposal for a law. Let me list them: the implementation of the European acquis into Slovenian legislation, which promotes more equal distribution of the obligation of protection and care between women and men; the regulation of rights for social parents, which regulates the adoption leave to the same extent as for parental leave for biological parents; the harmonisation of legislation with the European Social Charter and the exercise of rights under the Labour Law, which reduces the administrative burden for employers; the simplification of work in social work centres, where work at centres will be simpler and cheaper; and the simplification of the content of rights and deadlines. Since the existing parental protection policy in Slovenia is effective in terms of women's employment, but not in terms of reducing inequalities between women and men in the field of professional and family life coordination, I believe that the proposed changes to the law are more than welcome. I am confident that the amendments to the proposed legislation will have many positive effects in this area. I will therefore support the conclusion that the Proposal for a Law on Parental Protection and Family Benefits is appropriate for further consideration. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u142	Thank you very much for your word, Madam President. Dear Members, distinguished Members! The current Law on the Protection of Documentary and Archive Material and Archives provides comprehensively for the protection of documentary and archival material in physical and electronic form. The law was adopted in 2006 and very much intervened in the practice of monitoring documentary and archival materials so far, and stressed the concern about the preservation of the material even before it was downloaded into the archives. After seven years of implementation of the provisions of the law and administrative practice, it has proved necessary to correct and update certain segments of the law, to remove certain administrative obstacles and to bring it into line with the provisions of certain applicable system laws. Since 2009, expert discussions and preparation of changes and corrections to those solutions in the law dealing with electronic archiving or digital material, access to archival material, self-protection of archival material and other minor additions to public-law obligations have been conducted. Since spring 2012, a broad discussion has led to the preparation of the current proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Law on the Protection of Documentary and Archive Material. In the first round, the preparation was led by the Archives of the Republic of Slovenia, which carried out a number of coordinations and discussions with stakeholders concerned in the preparation of the amendment. In the second round, the Ministry of Culture appointed a special expert commission in spring 2013 with representatives of regional archives, the Archives of the Republic of Slovenia, the Archivan Society of Slovenia, the Ministry of Culture and the Ministry of the Interior. The Commission continued its work on the proposal prepared by the Archives of the Republic of Slovenia. In the first phase, it resubmitted an invitation to the archives, the Archivan Society of Slovenia, to representatives of ministries to submit comments or additions to the draft amendment of the law. The draft novel was then presented to the public on 29 May this year in the same form as it was prepared by the Archive in 2012, and a broad public debate was opened until 8 July of this year. On 4 June 2013, the proposal for a novel at its meeting was also addressed by the National Council for Culture. On 27 June this year, the Ministry of Culture and the Archives of the Republic of Slovenia also organized a round table to discuss the part of the novel which regulates electronic material and internal rules. A number of proposals were received through a wide range of expert and public hearings and subsequent cross-cutting coordination, but they did not intervene more extensively in the draft law and were examined and taken into account by the Ministry in large part. The changes in the novel relate mainly to the adoption and validation of internal archiving rules. Some administrative obstacles have been removed, for example, the law allows private law persons to use the instrument of internal archiving rules and does not oblige them to do so. It also introduces some rationalisation of procedures, verification of compliance of machinery with the requirements of the law. The law does not derogate from the existing principles of protection for public persons, nor does it abolish the validation of internal rules for each individual public legal person. The contents of the internal rules of all public legal persons will be pre-ordered by uniform technological requirements, which, by means of a proposal for a law, rise to the level of a sub-legislative act. It introduces a legal basis for the protection of the archival material of political parties and legal persons acting in the public interest and also regulates the possibility of protecting the archive material for public entities. The law also regulates the conditions of access to archival material, the deadlines for inaccessibility, exceptional access and the exceptional extension of the deadlines for the non-availability of archival material. According to the proposal, access to archival material will be regulated in the manner proposed by the Office of the Information Officer to bring the archival legislation into line with the legislation governing the protection of personal data. A broad professional and public debate has thus reached a compromise, the result of which is the present proposal of the law. This represents a crucial point between the needs of the archival profession and the requirements for removing obstacles on the other hand. On the other hand, between the requirements for widest accessibility on the one hand and the protection of personal data on the other. Therefore, honourable Members and honourable Members, I call on you to support the proposed law in anticipation of a useful and professional debate. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u146	Thank you for your word, Madam Vice President. Nice greetings to the members of the Ministry, honourable Members and Members of Parliament. The Law on the Protection of Documentary and Archive Material and Archives fully regulates the protection of documentary and archival material in physical and electronic form. It is a very complex matter, with direct implementation laid down by law and administrative practice showing that certain amendments or additions to certain articles are necessary. It is worth noting that the proposed amendment does not derogate from the principles of the applicable law, i.e. the principle of maintaining documentary material or the usefulness of its content, the principles of secrecy, integrity, the principle of accessibility, the principle of the safety of the cultural monument. At the same time, it further ensures respect for the principle of constitutionality and legality, the principle of proportionality and the principles of safeguarding the interests of national security or national sovereignty. The most important change compared to the regulation so far is the law on the regulation of the adoption and validation of internal rules. The law currently in force has committed itself to drawing up internal rules for both a public person and a person of private law, and it has unduly infringed on their rights. Amendments will allow private individuals to use the internal rules instrument and will not be obliged to do so. The next major amendment to the proposed law is the changes related to the regulation of access to public archival material, which contains classified information under each secret information law, tax secrecy and personal data. Secret information must be given consistent protection at a time when documents live, are used and serve the purpose for which they were created. After a relatively long period since the creation of the documents, the reasonable interest that these data are still protected from the public will cease to be reasonable, and confidentiality will normally lose its meaning in a material sense. The legal basis for the protection of the material of political parties and associations or private persons acting in the public interest is also introduced. Documentary material of these will have the property of archival material. In the DeSUS Group, we are pleased to note that the bill is fully aligned with the professional, information officer, the Intelligence and Security Service and also with the Ombudsman. Members and Members of the DeSUS Group will be supported by the Proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on the Protection of Documentary and Archive Material and Archives. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u152	Thank you for your word, Madam President. Your Honor! Since 2009, expert discussions have been under way and the preparation of amendments to the individual solutions of the Law on the Protection of Documentary and Archive Material and the archives of 2006, which fully regulate the protection of documentary and archival material in physical and electronic form. So far, for various reasons, all efforts to regulate open issues have been unsuccessful. The proposal of the novel, which is before us today, has been prepared since spring 2012 in a broad professional and public debate. Many comments and suggestions from the professional and interested public are included in the present bill. The amendments seen by the proponents as most important concern the adoption and validation of internal rules, and the amendments also refer to the regulation of the possibility of self-protection of archival material for public entities, the arrangements for the conditions of access to archival material, deadline access, exceptional access, and the exceptional extension of the time limits for the availability of archival material, and the arrangements for the protection of the material of political parties and private legal persons acting in the public interest. When it comes to the assessment and validation of internal rules and accreditation, it is necessary to simplify procedures as the current law defines these procedures as mandatory for all, including the private sector. He was overburdened by this. Public entities will still be required to adopt internal rules, but they will not need to be placed in a state archive approval and will have to comply with the new regulation or with uniform technological requirements. The provisions in force concerning the availability of archival material, despite the other well-designed access regime, are found to be unnecessary in some cases. Thus, in some cases they allow for the wide disclosure of data obtained or generated in the implementation of public authorities' intelligence and counter-intelligence activities, as well as personal data. The Constitutional Court has repeatedly stated that interference with constitutional rights and freedoms is permissible only if the restriction of the right is followed by a constitutionally permissible objective and the resources are legally permissible, which is appropriate for the legitimacy test and, at the same time, the proportionality test has to stop. The current regulation is questionable from the point of view of the proportionality test. The full opening of archives is not the only option, but the pursued objective, which is primarily a historical study, can also be ensured by a slightly more selective definition of access to archives. This is also permitted by international instruments. Access to archive material is therefore regulated taking into account both the public interest and the constitutionally guaranteed human rights and security aspects. Uniform conditions of accessibility for all archive material and equal protection of sensitive personal data shall be introduced before and after the date of 17 May 1990. However, a key part of these amendments is a provision which makes it very clear that only individual specific documents containing such data, and not a broader unit of consolidated documents, such as a file or case, may not be available. For all researchers, journalists, and publicists, this is a very welcome solution, so that they will be able to get to know much of the material, but they will still have the possibility of a comprehensive view or excellent access to the material when they meet the conditions. Furthermore, the paper introduces changes in the own protection of archival material for public legal persons who can, on the basis of certain conditions, provide for their own protection of archival material. Such authorisation may only be granted on the basis of sound professional and technical reasons. Due to the significant historical value of the materials of political parties and societies acting in the public interest, it will be possible to re-engage their documentary material in order to ensure that this material is adequately secured in the form of storage with the creator himself or with the relevant archive. The proposed novel, which has undergone a number of treatments and coordinations, will, in our opinion, allow for a quality professional work of the public archival service, easier cooperation with archive materials creators and appropriate and effective processes of handling archival and documentary material. We are therefore of the view in the Group of Positive Slovenia that the proposal for a new law is suitable for further consideration. Thank you.

In the Group of Members, we agree with the amended text relating to the internal rules and, following a new one, allowing individuals of private law to use the instrument of internal rules and does not oblige them to do so, as laid down in the current law. With regard to the delivery of archive material to the archive, the amendment of the law brings some changes resulting from the conclusion of the profession that the material must be delivered 30 years after the creation and also brings some exceptional extensions under the agreement between the competent archive and the public legal person. These more controversial changes have apparently come into the text in the last period of law-making and not from the profession, but from the current policy. The law already in Article 65, paragraph 3, protects the availability of sensitive personal data in the material of social policy organisations and intelligence services for persons who did not bear public functions. However, the existing law does not provide for such exceptions for persons who have held public office. In addition to extending the accessibility deadlines, this exception is omitted in the new law and, in our opinion, it closes access to those archival materials that allow insight into human rights violations prior to the establishment of the Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia on 17 May 1990. This is clearly in the political interest of the current authority. This also seems to be related to the European Union's requirements with the Croatian "lex Perkovic" and such possible implications in the case of Slovenia. Although the archival material of the intelligence services or the political police of the former country, which systematically violated human rights, has been pretty consistently destroyed, the fear of exposing these violations in the new law remains constant. Apparently, the pillars of the current authorities are disrupting the problem and, by chance, from secondary archival sources, the disclosures obtained. Such as the disclosure of how the party and the then government, when democratic political parties were formed, sought to infiltrate the active members of the State Security Service in the lead of the new parties. This is what we are taught by the current role of activity and the position of individuals who at that time, under the cover of democratic orientation, plunged into the leadership or backdrop of new parties. The trials of these restrictions are, in an ad hoc manner, contained in the amended Article 65 of the Law, and neither for the purposes of historical and public science do they adequately address the exceptions provided for in Article 66 of the Law. The public person who has supplied the archive material thus allows a discretionary right to extend the availability deadlines. The disguised possibility of restricting access is also finely introduced through a new own-preservation institute, which we support. The user of the material in his own storage must sign the declaration concerning the restriction referred to in Articles 65 and 66 and thus access to certain materials may be permanently restricted. Otherwise, in the SDS, we agree that personal data are the material to be given high protection and tender treatment. It is completely illogical that the issue of this law is regulated and limited by another law. Restrictions on access to archives are therefore quietly introduced. As a novelty, the law also provides for the storage of archival material by political parties who have the status of private archive material under this law. In the SDS Group, we find that this law is also one of those who sometimes misuse very complicated terminology and also does not tend to be easily understood, which is an obstacle for users, because it allows different interpretations. ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u155	Thank you very much. The word shall be given by the Group of the Social Democrats and the Magi. Majda Potter.
ParlaMint-SI_2013-11-22-SDZ6-Redna-19.ana.u156	Thank you for your word, Madam Vice President. Mr. Secretary of State with fellow Members, Members and Members of Parliament! Documentary and archival material is invaluable to the preservation of historical facts and represents an important heritage of each country. However, life in the digital age offers a number of challenges in this area, but also some traps. Thus, the risk of material being lost is significantly greater for material in electronic form than for that in writing. The possibilities for intervention are also greater than those for written material. If the traceability of the document is not clear, there may be reasonable doubts as to the authenticity of the material. Normative changes in the handling of documentary and archival material should therefore, in the view of the Group of Social Democrats, strengthen the mechanisms that will ensure the highest possible degree of credibility of documents produced in the digital environment. It is further necessary to ensure uniformity of standards that will allow documents to be available and readable in future programming environments. The draft law before us is partly following those starting points, but it is nevertheless worth noting some of the provisions of the proposal which, in the view of the Group of Social Democrats in the wake of the circumstances, may lead to shortcomings in the handling of documentary and archival material. There is a fear that because of debirocratisation or often called removal of administrative barriers, the strength of the protection of material in the digital environment is weakening. This is highlighted by part of the professional public, which was also observed by the National Council for Culture at the time of its consideration of the draft bill when it called on the Ministry of Culture to work towards the greatest possible consensus of the professional public on e-construction solutions and internal rules. Therefore, we want the applicant to re-examine the individual solutions of these two important areas for consideration in the home working body. In the Group of Social Democrats, we draw particular attention to provisions that reduce the role of the Republic of Slovenia's Archives and loosen the provisions on internal rules. The archive will no longer have the authority to confirm internal rules or public law. This will lose the opportunity to ensure professional verification of whether the internal rules are in accordance with the applicable legislation, whether electronic documents will have legal value when captured and, most importantly, whether they can be loaded into a form that will allow permanent storage. There is a serious fear that the material will be handed over to the archive in unreadable formats. In order to avoid this, we propose that the validation of the internal rules of public law remain the competence of the archive or that the sub-legislative area be regulated in a clear way. In the Group of Social Democrats, we hope that this time the debate on archives will go beyond the usual over-politicisation of the availability and accessibility of material created during the period of the former state. The applicant shall introduce into the law the protection of the most sensitive personal data in the materials of that period, and it shall be noted that the personal data of the perpetrators of political offences or offences shall be made available without restriction. In view of the other provisions that extend access to archival material and make it possible to have exceptional access to classified and personal data, even though the deadlines for non-accessibility have not yet expired, we propose that the proposal of the members of the archival commission be defined in law in the Parliamentary Group of Social Democrats. The current way allows each government to draw up its own composition, which is not appropriate in view of the broad competences of this Commission with regard to the decision on the availability of individual materials. In our opinion, it would make sense to establish a composition of a commission to which members, experts would propose institutions that enjoy broad confidence, such as the Ombudsman, the Information Officer, the SAZU and so on. In the Parliamentary Group of Social Democrats, we will support the proposal for a new law in the first reading. Thank you.
